## 19_Part_XIII_Telemetry_Write_Then_Read.md

**16.**
- **Where**: 19, Part lead
- **Text**: "Part IX introduced the sensor platform"
- **Verdict**: INCONSISTENT
- **Evidence**: The sensor platform is Part VIII (12_Part_VIII_Sensors_and_AI_Platforms.md). Part IX is Ribbit Wire / BLDC-1 / FNWP.
- **Fix**: "Part VIII introduced the sensor platform"

**17.**
- **Where**: 19, 42b "The one-shot writer"
- **Text**: "tc -s qdisc show dev ham0 | metric_upsert.sh LinkStats HAM"
- **Verdict**: WRONG (cannot work)
- **Evidence**: `usr/local/bin/metric_upsert.sh:43` is `METRIC_JSON="${3:?jsonData required}"`, and the script never reads stdin. The piped form exits with "jsonData required". The same bug is in `usr/local/bin/semantic_monitor.sh`.
- **Fix**: `metric_upsert.sh LinkStats HAM "$(tc -s qdisc show dev ham0 | jq -Rs '{qdisc:.}')"`

**18.**
- **Where**: 19, 42b "Asynchronous by construction"
- **Text**: "Event-driven one-shot. A unit that fires when something happens rather than on a clock — post-merge metrics run once after a merge completes, gated on sentinels for merge completion and a known data host"
- **Verdict**: STALE
- **Evidence**: `usr/local/bin/frognet_build_release.sh:165` ([DEAD_UNITS_REMOVED_V1]) says frognet-post-merge-metrics was dropped on 2026-08-29. Its ExecStart, `/usr/local/bin/frognet_post_merge_metrics.sh`, does not exist. The event-driven producer that does exist is `emit_routes_snapshot.sh`, called at `usr/local/bin/mergeHostsAndResolv.bash:326`.
- **Fix**: "Event-driven one-shot. A producer that fires when something happens rather than on a clock — the route snapshot is written once at the end of every merge (emit_routes_snapshot.sh, called by mergeHostsAndResolv.bash)."

**19.**
- **Where**: 19, 42b, "the whole thing is wrapped so it cannot hurt the node"
- **Text**: "A snapshot that throws is reported and skipped so one bad metric does not cost the batch"
- **Verdict**: WRONG (partly)
- **Evidence**: In `opt/frognet_semantic/proxy/proxy_metrics.py:1257-1303`:
  - Only pipeline stats, Apache workers and routing resilience have their own try blocks.
  - Endpoints, the per-peer rows, topology, link quality and engine share one outer try. If one of them throws, the rest of the collection stops and the items gathered so far are sent.
- **Fix**: "A pipeline, Apache or resilience snapshot that throws is reported and skipped; if one of the core snapshots throws, collection stops there and what was gathered is still sent. A batch that fails to send is logged and dropped."

**20.**
- **Where**: 19, 42c opening
- **Text**: "Start it with python3 -m frognet_monitor."
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: There is no `frognet_monitor` package. The package is `usr/local/bin/frognet_monitor_py/`, and the launcher `usr/local/bin/frognet_monitor` runs `cd /usr/local/bin; python3 -m frognet_monitor_py`. The chapter's own oracle command uses `frognet_monitor_py`. A separate C++ monitor, `usr/local/bin/frognet_monitor_cpp`, builds with CMake to `build/frognet_monitor` and takes the subcommands identity, hosts, sensors and read.
- **Fix**: "Start it with frognet_monitor (it runs python3 -m frognet_monitor_py from /usr/local/bin)."

**21.**
- **Where**: 19, 42c "The two calls"
- **Text**: "Behind that name are two tables. sensors holds the metadata"
- **Verdict**: INCONSISTENT (repeats the previous paragraph's opening)
- **Fix**: "sensors holds the metadata — SensorID, SensorName, SensorType — and sensor_data holds the payload…"

**22.**
- **Where**: 19, 42c "The two calls"
- **Text**: "The Monitor resolves nothing itself and hardwires nothing; it asks for the name and lets the pond answer."
- **Verdict**: WRONG (Python monitor)
- **Evidence**:
  - The Python monitor does `urllib.request.urlopen("http://databasehost.frognet/api.php…")` (`frognet_monitor_py/sensors.py`, `discovery.py:31`). That passes the name to the system resolver, then connects to the resulting 10/8 address on port 80. `etc/setup_iptables:79` redirects that connection into the local proxy.
  - So the name is resolved, by the resolver, on the monitor's behalf.
  - The C++ monitor does what the sentence says: it connects to 127.0.0.1:80 with `Host: databasehost.frognet` (`frognet_monitor_cpp/src/http.cpp:74,106`).
- **Fix**: "The Monitor hardwires nothing. It asks for the name databasehost.frognet on port 80, and the local proxy carries the request to wherever the pond's data host is now (the C++ monitor sends it to 127.0.0.1:80 with the name in the Host header)."

**23.**
- **Where**: 19, 42c "Is a merge running?"
- **Text**: "If the sentinel is already there the first press refuses and says how old it is; a second press goes anyway."
- **Verdict**: WRONG (incomplete)
- **Evidence**: `frognet_monitor_py/main_loop.py:399-406` forces only while the refusal note is still standing, which is `MERGE_NOTE_S = 6.0` seconds (main_loop.py:346). A second press after that refuses again.
- **Fix**: "…a second press within six seconds, while the refusal is still on screen, goes anyway."

**24.**
- **Where**: 19, 42c "Never block the screen"
- **Text**: "The main loop only draws and reads keys — it never waits on a socket."
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: `frognet_monitor_py/main_loop.py:425` calls `fetch_sensor_detail(sname)` synchronously when Enter is pressed in the sensor overlay. That is two `api_request` calls of up to 3 s each (`sensors.py`), so the draw loop can block for up to 6 s. The `r` refresh in the JSON view does use a thread (line 449).
- **Fix**: Move the Enter fetch onto a thread in `_handle_sensor_input`. Or say "…it never waits on a socket, except when Enter opens one sensor's JSON, which is fetched on the main loop."

**25.**
- **Where**: 19, 42c "Never block the screen"
- **Text**: "Because curses owns the terminal, nothing prints."
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: `frognet_monitor_py/identity.py:146-148`. When `/etc/hosts` cannot be read, `_hosts_lookup` calls `print("[monitor] cannot read %s …")` to stdout, onto the curses screen, before it raises.
- **Fix**: Route that message through `trace()`. Or say "Because curses owns the terminal, diagnostics go to /tmp/frognet_monitor.log; the one exception is an unreadable /etc/hosts, which prints a line before the reader raises."

**26.**
- **Where**: 19, closing oracle block and the paragraph after it
- **Text**: "=== 10 passed, 0 failed ===" / "Ten goals, pinning the sentinel as the source, the three-state reading, the age formatting, the caching, both refusal paths on M, and the indicator's place on the title line."
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: I re-ran `frognet_monitor_py/test_merge_oracle.py` on a copy. It prints 41 `PASS  G<n> …` lines and ends with `ALL PASS`. There are twelve goals, G1–G12. G11 is the force-on-second-press window and G12 is that every exit from `start_merge` is traced. The string "passed, 0 failed" is not in the file.
- **Fix**:
  ```
  $ PYTHONPATH=/usr/local/bin python3 -m frognet_monitor_py.test_merge_oracle
    PASS  G1 …
    …
    PASS  G12 the successful launch is traced too

  ALL PASS
  ```
  "Twelve goals: the sentinel as the source, the three-state reading, the age formatting, the caching, both refusal paths on M, the force-on-second-press window, that every way out of start_merge is traced, and the indicator's place on the title line."

---

Not book errors, but code that contradicts the settled no-fallback ruling the book has to agree with. Reported here so the editor knows the book is describing code accurately:
- `frognet_monitor_py/discovery.py:96-108` falls back from getHosts.php to /etc/hosts.
- `simulation/netns_backend.py:194` [WG_USERSPACE_FALLBACK_V1] uses wireguard-go when wireguard.ko is absent.
- `frognet_monitor_cpp/src/dashboard.cpp:72` claims "same order as the Python", which is false (see item 12).

Checked: 154 claims; 26 problems.

# Report H — Part XIV (Writing Your Own Handler), Part XV (The Communicator), against the 2026-10-11 tree

Conversation tools were available (recent_chats, conversation_search; a46794d6, a16e4802, a3c5615d, 24e6c9e1 lineage via
the 10-08 report). Code: TREE = /home/claude/tree1011. C = TREE/opt/frognet_semantic/ribbit/examples/communicator
(comms-app r3). A = TREE/opt/fdf/src/frognet-android (Communicator r5 inside the FrogNet app).

Overall: neither part file changed since 10-08. Every 10-08 problem in them still stands against today's code, and
the 10-10/10-11 work adds new ones: the Communicator now finds its servers through the pond's where cell (Android,
Windows bridge), the desktop is comms-app r3 with ringing/stamp, the phone has its own VP8/Opus sender. Part XV §46 is
REWRITE. §47–§51 need the corrections below; a full replacement for §46–§51 is at the end. §51a and §52 are history
and can stay apart from the two fixes listed.

---

## Part XIV

**Where**: 20_Part_XIV — §44 "Authority"
**Text**: "resolved by name as <game>.frognet"
**Verdict**: WRONG
**Evidence**: TREE/opt/frognet_semantic/ribbit/cpp/tests/reference/core/game_role.py:33 `ROLE_NAME = "boardgame"`; core/frognet_service_hosts.py:24,141 emits `<role>.frognet` → one pond-wide `boardgame.frognet`. game_role.py exists only as a test reference copy, not in core/.
**Fix**: "resolved by name as boardgame.frognet"

**Where**: 20_Part_XIV — §44 and §45 worked examples
**Text**: "POST <game>.frognet/api.php?entity=move&action=upsert_by_name"
**Verdict**: UNSUPPORTED
**Evidence**: No game engine anywhere in TREE (grep open_tables / game_base / GameCodex: nothing). boardgame is stripped by usr/local/bin/frognet_make_build_tar.sh:97,215; install_boardgamehost.sh is listed gone in frognet_prune_removed.sh:261. ribbit/cpp/tests/reference/daemon/daemon_metrics.py:26: `action=upsert_by_name` "doesn't exist". game_role.py:20: the proxy answers `POST /game` on port 80.
**Fix**: Label the requests illustrative: "Schematically, a move is a write such as …" (same for the `entity=game&action=list` example).

**Where**: 20_Part_XIV — §45 op:"join" example and "Join already works"
**Text**: "# the oracle above is now GREEN."
**Verdict**: UNSUPPORTED
**Evidence**: No open_tables() and no lobby oracle in TREE. 74115b3f (07-02): open_tables() was proven only against in-memory stores; production-store keys() and UI wiring were left owed. Nothing later records them done.
**Fix**: "open_tables() turns the oracle green against the engine; the production store and the lobby UI still have to be wired."

**Where**: 20_Part_XIV — §45 "The lesson"
**Text**: "the user-facing surface is built from — presence, chat, media, and games, each a handler over the one interface. That surface is the Communicator"
**Verdict**: STALE
**Evidence**: The Communicator in C has no games (no game code in C/src, C/python, A/app). Its presence and chat are tuples in comms-ram (C/src/comms/room.hpp:6-8; C/src/ram/comms_ram.cpp is a plain ribbit::RamHost "with no operations of its own"), and its media is the fnav wire to comms-media, not a handler.
**Fix**: "The engine you just extended is one handler; the Communicator in the next part is presence, chat and calls written as rows in its own Region, with media on its own wire. It carries no games."

**Where**: 20_Part_XIV — §44 after the first worked example
**Text**: "it is the same loop the Communicator uses for a call: resolve the role name, read your region to render, write your own intent."
**Verdict**: STALE
**Evidence**: The Communicator resolves no role name. comms-app takes `--ram HOST:PORT --media HOST:PORT` (C/src/app/main.cpp:7,822); on a pond those come from the where cell (TREE/opt/fdf/bin/fdf-comms.sh; A/app/.../MemberService.kt:217-229; opt/fdf/windows/bridge/fdf-bridge.cmd:8).
**Fix**: "it is the same loop the Communicator uses for a call: read your Region to render, write your own rows."

## Part XV — opening

**Where**: 21_Part_XV — opening paragraph
**Text**: "It grows — a bundle installs and the node can do something new."
**Verdict**: STALE
**Evidence**: The Communicator is no bundle. On a node it is two servers started by fdf-comms.sh (opt/fdf/bin/fdf-comms.sh), binaries in /opt/fdf/comms (comms-ram, comms-media).
**Fix**: "It grows — fdf-comms.sh starts two servers on a node, says where they are in the pond, and every member can call."

Now: comms-app (C++/Qt 6) from C/build.sh on Linux/Pi, C/build-windows.ps1 (Visual Studio + vcpkg; C/WINDOWS.md) on Windows, python/comms_app.py (PySide6). **Verdict**: STALE

**Verdict**: STALE

**Where**: §46 oracle blocks
**Text**: "$ python3 test_presence_freshness_oracle.py" / "test_recv_only_vdec_oracle.py" / "test_lobby_discovery_oracle.py"
**Verdict**: UNSUPPORTED
**Evidence**: None of the three files exists in TREE. The shipped tests are C/tests/* (test_room, test_room_watch, test_ring, test_feed_view, test_call, test_call_audio, test_media_rates, test_media_link, test_bottom …) and A/communicator-test/run_tests.sh.
**Fix**: see REWRITE (cites the real tests).

**Where**: §46 Part II "Starting and joining a call"
**Text**: "allocates a connector for this session — its own ports, because a dozen simultaneous calls cannot share one"
**Verdict**: WRONG
**Evidence**: comms-media listens on one port, 8994. Each connection's first frame is KIND_PLANE = plane 'A'/'V' + 8-byte teardown tag + session (C/src/fnav/frame.hpp:25; C/src/comms/call.cpp:63-73); fan is per session (C/src/media/server.cpp:66-72 peers_of). A call is a `CallsAnnounce` row at `user:<name>` carrying media host:port (room.cpp:213-241); invitations are `CallsInvitations` at `user:<callee>`.
**Fix**: see REWRITE.

**Where**: §46 Part II, second paragraph
**Text**: "a second, dedicated socket per leg, FNWP-1 framed"
**Verdict**: WRONG (new)
**Evidence**: The media planes are the fnav wire, `[len u32][kind u8][srclen u16][src][payload]` (C/src/fnav/frame.hpp:6-9), not FNWP's length/opcode/seq. FNWP/BLDC-1 is what the room's memory traffic uses (comms-ram is a ribbit::RamHost; third_party/ribbit/cpp/include/fnwp_engine.hpp, FULL/DIFF/SAME stats :124).
**Fix**: "two dedicated TCP connections per participant, audio and video, in fnav frames"

**Where**: §46 Part II, second paragraph; Part I install paragraph
**Text**: "mixes all the audio into one, and fans the result back to each person minus their own face and voice" / "builds the combined video"
**Verdict**: WRONG
**Evidence**: Server::fan_audio (server.cpp:105-114) forwards each audio frame unchanged to every other participant; each client mixes (C/src/comms/audio.*; A/core/Mixer.java). No composite video; video is passed through or transcoded per viewer (server.cpp fan_video / tx_worker).
**Fix**: see REWRITE.

**Where**: §46 Part III and caveat
**Text**: "A game is one tuple: a board, its seats, and whose turn it is"
**Verdict**: STALE
**Evidence**: No games in C or A.
**Fix**: delete Part III; one sentence in the REWRITE points to Part XIV.

## §47 The Wire Under the Call

**Where**: §47 first sentence; §51 first sentence
**Text**: "The ladder in Part VII tells you which rung a call should be on." / "Part VII describes the rungs."
**Verdict**: INCONSISTENT
**Evidence**: Part VII is Connecting Across the Internet (10_Part_VII…). The ladder is Part X §31-33 (14_Part_X…).
**Fix**: "Part X" in both places.

**Where**: §47 "Send or drop"; §48 opening
**Text**: "SO_SNDLOWAT, which is 2048 bytes on Linux and 1 on Windows"
**Verdict**: WRONG
**Evidence**: Linux fixes SO_SNDLOWAT at 1 (measured 10-08); 2048 is the BSD/macOS default. The C++ never reads it; it asks SIOCOUTQ against SO_SNDBUF (C/src/fnav/plane.cpp:79-86, net.cpp:37-39).
**Fix**: "select reports a socket writable when the send buffer has SOME room — not room for your frame."

**Where**: §47 "What about the buffer query"
**Text**: "the media relay does exactly that. It is not portable — fcntl and termios do not exist on Windows"
**Verdict**: STALE
**Evidence**: Plane::send_room (plane.cpp:79-86) is in every sender, client and server: SO_SNDBUF − SIOCOUTQ, and a shortage is believed only when the queue is past 3/4 of the cap. On Windows there is no SIOCOUTQ: net_unsent returns −1, would-block decides, SNDBUF is 64 KiB (plane.hpp:36-41). The halving survives in the segmenter: whole_frame_max caps a whole frame at SNDBUF/2 (segment.cpp:20-25). fcntl/termios is the Python lineage.
**Fix**: "On Linux every sender computes free space as SO_SNDBUF minus SIOCOUTQ, and believes a shortage only when the queue is past three quarters of the buffer, because the two numbers are not in the same units. Windows has no SIOCOUTQ: there the socket's would-block decides, and the buffer is 64 KiB to bound how much stale picture can wait in it."

**Where**: §47 "Why the keyframe is broken up"
**Text**: "audio still arrives at a hundred and twenty kilobits a second"
**Verdict**: STALE
**Evidence**: Opus 24 kb/s, 16 kHz mono, 20 ms blocks (C/src/comms/audio.hpp:26-29; A/AudioEngine.java:31); the server budgets 32 kb/s with framing (server.hpp:103). 120 kb/s was PCM-era fnav.
**Fix**: "audio still arrives — Opus at 24 kb/s — while the video is visibly degraded"

**Where**: §47 "Send or drop"
**Text**: "There is no send queue, no retry buffer, and no pacing thread."
**Verdict**: WRONG (detail)
**Evidence**: plane.hpp:7-8,42-44 and plane.cpp:103-106: audio is retried twice 8 ms apart and a keyframe three times before it is dropped; other video never. A committed frame is finished within 250 ms or padded.
**Fix**: "There is no send queue and no pacing thread. An inter frame gets one try. A keyframe gets three and an audio block three, 8 ms apart, because one would-block is not a verdict."

## §49 The Frame That Must Not Be Dropped

**Where**: §49 para 4
**Text**: "the level field, which carries a value of 0 to 7"
**Verdict**: WRONG
**Evidence**: frame.hpp:48 `uint32_t level; // rung 0..8`, KEYFRAME_BIT 0x80000000 (frame.hpp:32). L8 = 1920x1080 (C/src/media/transcode.cpp:18).
**Fix**: "which carries a rung of 0 to 8"

**Where**: §49 para 5
**Text**: "A held keyframe is allowed to overdraw its budget."
**Verdict**: STALE
**Evidence**: comms-media has no budget bucket. A viewer's cap picks the size encoded for it (target_for_cap, server.cpp:116-121); a held keyframe is kept at most 3 s (server.hpp:91 HELD_KEY_MAX_S; server.cpp deliver_video_locked). The overdraw survives only in the sender's own throttle, the bandwidth slider: keyframes always pass and the bucket goes negative (C/src/comms/call.cpp:262-267).
**Fix**: "The media server has no bucket: a viewer's cap chooses the picture size it is sent, and a held keyframe waits at most three seconds. The overdraw lives in the sender's throttle: a keyframe always goes, the budget goes negative, and inter frames repay it."

## §50 Evidence, Not Arithmetic

**Where**: §50 paras 5-8
**Text**: "it reports the count upstream once a second. The sender steps the ceiling down immediately"
**Verdict**: STALE
**Evidence**: comms-media sends a sender only KIND_KEYREQ (server.cpp ask_key); it never sends KIND_BACKPRESSURE or KIND_AUDIO_BACKPRESSURE (grep: only the receiver at call.cpp:168 handles the latter). Each viewer's size is chosen at the server from its measured link: control_tick, 2 s windows, delivered/aimed < 0.80 for 2 windows → down one size and that size held off 15 s doubling to 300 s; up one size after 3 good windows, not within 45 s of the last change (server.hpp:105-108; server.cpp:140-196). FNAV-SPEC.md:200-202 "The relay's drops do not steer the sender … The rewrite's media server does exactly this."
**Fix**: see §50 in the replacement.

**Where**: §50 para 8
**Text**: "A client publishes its own bandwidth setting, so it can cap its ceiling from that number before the first frame goes out."
**Verdict**: STALE
**Evidence**: FNAV-SPEC.md:168: [SELF_CAP_FROM_LINK_V1] "is gone". The sender's ceiling is set by its source size only (call.cpp:41-44 ceiling_for). The MediaSpeed row caps what the viewer RECEIVES (server.cpp set_cap_row).
**Fix**: "A client publishes its bandwidth setting as MediaSpeed, and the media server serves that viewer within it from the first frame. The sender's own ceiling is what its camera can fill; the bearer finds the rest."

## §51 How the Ladder Actually Decides

**Where**: §51 "Down — any one of four"
**Text**: "they are checked in this order:"
**Verdict**: WRONG
**Evidence**: Bearer::sample (C/src/fnav/bearer.cpp:31-35) checks audio shed FIRST, then >3 consecutive dirty samples (a drop OR a backlog of 8), then >5 drops in a closed second, then the frame-rate floor. Thresholds match. Same in A/core/Bearer.java.
**Fix**: put "Audio shed by the wire" first, and say "samples carrying drops (or a backlog of eight frames)".

**Where**: §51 "The floor is a voice"
**Text**: "Congestion walks the ladder down to the protected audio rung and stops there."
**Verdict**: STALE (incomplete)
**Evidence**: Below L5 the picture is not shed; it shrinks — 640x360, 480x360, 320x240, 160x120 — only when the wire refuses frames, one step per 2 s, and grows back after 3 clean 2 s windows; audio only comes only after 160x120 is refused, until the ladder is back at L5 (call.cpp:233-245,299-318 [BOTTOM_RUNG_SHRINKS_V1]; tests/test_bottom).
**Fix**: in the replacement.

**Where**: §51 WORKED EXAMPLE and following
**Text**: "987 kbps    held           no visible change"
**Verdict**: STALE (undated)
**Evidence**: The walk is the August fnav.py run (38223476, 1f49d502). Today's rungs are L7 1280x720 1.2 Mb/s, L6 854x480 600 kb/s, L5 640x360 300 kb/s gray (transcode.cpp:18-20); 850 kb/s would not hold L7 now.
**Fix**: head it "August 2026, the Python fnav Communicator:" and keep the figures.

**Where**: §51 "a ceiling slider and a jitter control"
**Text**: "a ceiling slider and a jitter control, the second adding frame noise in milliseconds"
**Verdict**: STALE
**Evidence**: main.cpp:449-460: Bandwidth (continuous, logarithmic, 32 kb/s–4 Mb/s, unlimited at the far right, applied after it rests 200 ms) and Jitter (0–500 ms). Bandwidth is the participant's link in both directions: it throttles the upload (call.cpp set_throttle) AND writes MediaSpeed (main.cpp:636). Jitter: a receive DelayLine per plane plus a sleep before each send (call.cpp:107-130,261).
**Fix**: "a bandwidth slider (your link, both directions) and a jitter slider (0 to 500 ms, both directions)"

**Where**: §51 "The rule itself, in full" and "What it does when you drag it"
**Text**: "def derive_rate(state, ladder, current=None):" / "The call has one control: a speed setting. Drag it down and both ends move"
**Verdict**: STALE
**Evidence**: derive_rate exists nowhere in TREE. FNAV-SPEC.md:289 "the derivation itself is replaced by decision 2" (one upload; the media server converts down per viewer). Dragging bandwidth lowers your upload (your bearer steps down, so others see a smaller picture of you) and your MediaSpeed (comms-media transcodes what you receive); nobody else's rate is derived from it. Also contradicts §51's own "two controls".
**Fix**: present derive_rate as the August design the rewrite superseded, or delete both sections; replacement text in §51 below.

## §51a / §52

**Where**: §51a "Reading a socket"
**Text**: "[THE_BUFFER_IS_THE_LATENCY_V1]256 KiB at 30 KB/s is 8.5 seconds"
**Verdict**: STALE (detail)
**Evidence**: plane.hpp:36-41: SNDBUF is now 128 KiB (Linux) and 64 KiB (Windows) for exactly this reason.
**Fix**: append "; the buffer is now 128 KiB on Linux and 64 KiB on Windows."

**Where**: §52 last paragraph
**Text**: "The Communicator is valuable because it exercised all of those concerns in one program"
**Verdict**: STALE (detail)
**Evidence**: The rewrite's memory side uses Ribbit RAM over FNWP/BLDC-1; its media wire is fnav, not FNWP.
**Fix**: "The Communicator is valuable because it exercised all of those concerns in one program — its rows over Ribbit, BLDC-1 and FNWP, its media on a wire of its own — and exposed where the original boundaries were wrong."

## REPLACEMENT — Part XV opening, §46, §47–§51 (grounded in C and A as of 2026-10-11)

> # Part XV The Communicator
>
> Every term of this book at once, in one application. The Communicator is alive — it finds its servers in the pond's
> memory instead of being told an address. It has memory — every participant publishes what it alone knows, and
> reads everyone else's. It learns — its rows cross the wire as differences against what both ends hold. It grows —
> one script on a node starts its servers and says where they are, and every member can call. And it is wrong in
> places, which is the last term and the reason the next part exists. It is a very complicated Hello, World, and its
> job is to touch every layer so the layers get tested.
>
> ### 46. The Communicator
>
> WATCH
> The Communicator — the flagship application, and the ladder on camera
>
> The Communicator is a video phone with a lobby and chat. It is an example program, not the product: the worked
> proof that a Ribbit Region, the FNWP wire with BLDC-1 under it, and Song of the Frogs policy compose into an
> application a person opens, and a program you can read end to end to learn how to build your own. It was rewritten
> in C++ in October 2026; the Python Communicator, its games and its :9000 media host are gone.
>
> #### The pieces
>
> Four programs, in ribbit/examples/communicator:
>
> - comms-ram, on TCP 8800: the Communicator's memory. It is a plain Ribbit RAM host with no operations of its own.
>   Presence, chat, calls and media speeds are rows in it.
> - comms-media, on TCP 8994: the media server. Every call meets there. It forwards each participant's audio to the
>   others in the call, and passes each participant's video through or transcodes it down for a viewer who cannot
>   take it.
> - comms-app: the window (C++, Qt 6). Lobby on the left, the call in the centre, chat on the right.
> - comms-feed: an unattended camera or file. It shows in the lobby as *name.
>
> python/comms_app.py is a second implementation of the same contract (PySide6). It shares nothing with the C++ but
> the rows in comms-ram, the wire to comms-media and the rules, and the two meet in one lobby and one call.
>
> On the phone the Communicator is a screen of the FrogNet app, CommunicatorActivity (r5). Its core — room, fnav
> wire, bearer, mixer — is Java, proven against the C++ and Python. It sends VP8 through MediaCodec from a Camera2
> picture drawn by GL into the encoder, and Opus through MediaCodec.
>
> #### Building and running it
>
> On Linux or a Pi, ./build.sh builds all four. On Windows, build-windows.ps1 builds comms-app and comms-feed with
> Visual Studio and vcpkg; comms-ram and comms-media run on the Linux side. The libraries are FFmpeg (libavcodec,
> libswscale, libavdevice), libvpx, libopus, PortAudio and Qt 6.
>
> On a FrogNet node, fdf-comms.sh starts comms-ram and comms-media and writes one cell in the pond's memory saying
> where they are: (pond, where, w:<guid>) {"comms": {"ram": "w:<guid>:8800", "media": "w:<guid>:8994"}}. The cell
> names the node by identity, not by address; each reader resolves it to the address that node has in the reader's
> own scope. The region removes the cell when the node's session ends, and fdf-comms.sh writes it again on the event
> that shows the node back. On the droplet, which has no .1, fdf-services.sh publishes the public address instead.
>
> Every program says what it is first: comms-app prints [app] build comms-app-2026-10-10-r3 and carries it in the
> window title; the phone logs comms-android-2026-10-11-r5.
>
> #### Finding your camera, microphone and speakers
>
> comms-app --list-devices prints the cameras as the exact --camera value to type — v4l2 on Linux, DirectShow on
> Windows — and the PortAudio table, with the index you give as --mic N and --speaker N. With no index the system
> default is used. A camera is asked for MJPEG: without it V4L2 hands out YUYV, and 1920x1080 YUYV is a USB 2.0 limit
> read as a machine limit. MJPEG's full-range levels are said, not assumed, so the picture is not washed out.
>
> A device's claim is not trusted. The microphone and speaker are opened mono at the device's own rate and resampled
> to and from the 16 kHz wire, and the rate the microphone really delivers is measured in its first seconds: an eMeet
> C950 claims 44.1 kHz and delivers about 16 kHz. A live camera has its own reader thread that keeps only the newest
> frame, so the picture does not fall behind the voice.
>
> You do not need a camera. Receiving needs none, only sending does. A camera that will not open is reported and the
> call goes on receiving.
>
> #### Finding the servers
>
> On the desktop you give the Communicator its name and the two addresses: --name, --ram HOST:8800,
> --media HOST:8994. On Windows, fdf-bridge.cmd ask comms prints the --ram and --media of the forward pond's
> Communicator. On the phone nothing is typed: the FrogNet member reads its pond's where cells for comms, resolves the
> identity in its own scope, and opens the Communicator with the two addresses and the device's name. If no member of
> the pond runs one, it says so and opens nothing.
>
> #### Presence, the lobby and chat
>
> Joining is not a request a server grants. There is no login and no session handed to you. Each participant writes
> one presence row, host:<ip>:presence:<me_id> {name, status, caps, ts}, and writes it again every 5 seconds. A row
> older than 15 seconds is gone. There is no logout and no delete: stale is gone. A comms-ram that restarts loses
> nobody, because the roster was never its; it is the set of fresh rows.
>
> Each participant keeps its own copy of the Communicator's state by one held read — "wake me when anything newer
> than write-order id N exists" — and answers the lobby, chat, offers and invitations from that copy. Its own writes
> go into the copy at once. A chat line reaches the other participant's copy in about a millisecond; an idle room
> makes a handful of requests a minute.
>
> Chat is one row per line, session:chat:<session>:<msg_id>, written by the sender and read in time order.
>
> #### Starting and joining a call
>
> You start a call by writing your CallsAnnounce row, at user:<your name>, listing the session and the media server's
> host and port. You invite someone by writing a CallsInvitations row at user:<their name>. An invitation rings only
> while it is under 60 seconds old and its caller still offers that session, so a call the caller gave up on stops
> ringing. Hanging up takes the session out of your rows.
>
> Joining is reading the offer and opening two TCP connections to the media server, one for audio and one for video.
> The first frame on each says which plane it is, an 8-byte teardown tag shared by the pair, and the session. Every
> call shares the one port; the server keeps their fans apart by session. Losing one plane closes its mate, and only
> its mate: half a caller is not a caller.
>
> The split is total. Control — who is here, what is offered, who is invited, each viewer's speed — is rows in
> comms-ram, crossing the FNWP wire full, then diff, then same. Audio and video never touch it: they go to comms-media
> in fnav frames, [len][kind][src][payload], send-or-drop.
>
> #### What the media server does
>
> Audio is forwarded unchanged to every other participant in the session, and each client mixes the voices it
> receives. Audio is never what is given up while video is still going to that viewer.
>
> Video is one upload. A viewer who can take it gets the sender's own stream, byte for byte. A viewer who cannot gets
> a smaller picture the server encodes — once per size, whatever the number of viewers at that size, on a worker that
> is not the thread reading the upload. A viewer is never sent anything larger than the upload. What a viewer can take
> is the smaller of two things: its MediaSpeed row, session:<session>:viewer:<me_id> {bps}, which the server holds a
> read on, and its measured link. One slow viewer never lowers anyone else.
>
> The server publishes what it is doing for each viewer as a MediaHold row every 2 seconds — pass-through or the size
> it sends, keyframe held, audio only — and the window shows it, including "No video — Audio only" instead of a frozen
> last frame.
>
> #### The tests
>
> The claims above are proven by the tests in the tree, and you can run them: test_room and test_room_watch on a real
> comms-ram (lobby, chat in order, MediaSpeed matched by identity, the held read), test_ring (rings only while
> offered), test_call and test_call_audio (two participants through comms-media, video at each other's size, tones in
> each other's speakers), test_feed_view (a receive-only viewer), test_media_rates and test_media_link (per-viewer
> sizes from the cap and from the measured link), test_bottom (the bottom walk). The phone's core runs the same vectors
> and a Robolectric run of the app's own screen against live servers (communicator-test/run_tests.sh). TESTING.md says
> how to run each.
>
> The Communicator carries no games. The game model is Part XIV's.
>
> ### 47. The Wire Under the Call
>
> The ladder in Part X tells you which rung a call should be on. This chapter is the socket under it.
>
> #### Send or drop — there is no queue
>
> Each plane is a non-blocking TCP socket, and the write is the test. There is no send queue and no pacing thread. An
> inter frame gets one try; if the socket will not take it, it is dropped and the next one is offered. A keyframe gets
> three tries and an audio block three, 8 ms apart, because one would-block is not a verdict.
>
> select() reports a socket writable when the send buffer has some room — not room for your frame. On Linux every
> sender computes free space as SO_SNDBUF minus SIOCOUTQ, and believes a shortage only when the queue is past three
> quarters of the buffer, because the two numbers are not in the same units: Linux doubles the buffer it reports and
> counts its own overhead in the queue. Windows has no SIOCOUTQ; there the socket's would-block decides, and the buffer
> is 64 KiB instead of 128 KiB, to bound how much stale picture can wait in it.
>
> One lock per socket serialises whole frames onto it. On the media server a viewer's socket is written in debt mode:
> a part-written frame's remainder is owed as abort padding and paid as the socket takes it, and new frames for that
> viewer are shed meanwhile. A viewer never makes a sender's thread wait.
>
> #### A partial write is not a dropped frame
>
> [keep the current section unchanged]
>
> #### Why the keyframe is broken up, and what rides in the gaps
>
> [keep the current text, with one change:] That is why audio still arrives — Opus at 24 kb/s — while the video is
> visibly degraded and may freeze outright. A frame larger than what the socket has been accepting in 20 ms (at most
> half the buffer) goes as VSEG pieces of 1368 bytes, the tunnels' MSS.
>
> WORKED EXAMPLE — Why not UDP, from somebody who shipped it [keep unchanged]
>
> #### Audio is not guaranteed, but it has priority — and one wire cannot deliver it
>
> [keep the current four paragraphs, replacing "The relay budgets the video plane and never touches the audio plane"
> with:] The media server sizes and sheds the video plane per viewer and never touches the audio plane.
>
> "No video" is a display state, not a teardown. [keep the rest unchanged]
>
> ### 48. A Frame That Did Not Arrive
>
> [keep, replacing the second paragraph with:] select reports a socket writable when the send buffer has SOME room,
> not room for your frame. The one call that gives a real number is SIOCOUTQ against SO_SNDBUF, and it is Linux-only.
>
> ### 49. The Frame That Must Not Be Dropped
>
> [keep paragraphs 1-3; in paragraph 4 read "which carries a rung of 0 to 8"; replace paragraph 5 with:]
>
> On the media server a held keyframe waits at most three seconds; then it is dropped and the viewer resumes at the
> next one. An unanchored viewer with nothing held asks for a keyframe: of the sender, for its own stream, at most
> once per 3 seconds; of the server's own encoder, for a transcoded size. The server has no budget bucket: a viewer's
> cap chooses the picture size it is sent, so the keyframe fits by construction. The overdraw lives in the sender's
> throttle, the bandwidth slider: a keyframe always goes, the budget goes negative, and the inter frames behind it
> repay the debt.
>
> ### 50. Evidence, Not Arithmetic
>
> The obvious way to choose a picture size is to compare its bitrate with the link rate. It is also wrong: there are
> links on which the arithmetic says video is impossible, and the wire was carrying it.
>
> [keep the paragraph on one lock per peer socket.]
>
> The media server keeps the evidence per viewer and acts on it for that viewer only; the sender is never told.
> Every 2 seconds it compares what it aimed at each viewer with what that viewer's socket took. Two windows below 80
> percent and the viewer steps down one size — from its transcoded size, or from the upload's own size, read from the
> rung in the upload's frame headers — and the size that failed is held off for 15 seconds, doubling to five minutes.
> Three good windows, and no change in the last 45 seconds, and it climbs one size; back at the upload's size it is
> passed the upload again. A window in which nothing was aimed is not a measurement.
>
> The sender has its own ladder, the bearer, and it governs only the upload: what the wire to the media server takes.
> The two never cross. A slow viewer gets a smaller picture; the sender keeps sending what its own link carries.
>
> The link rate is still worth knowing, as an opening value. A viewer's MediaSpeed row caps what it is sent from the
> first frame: the largest size whose bitrate plus 32 kb/s of audio fits, or audio only. The sender's own ceiling is
> what its camera can fill. Where a call SETTLES is decided by the evidence.
>
> [keep the last paragraph, "There is one thing this chapter should not leave you expecting".]
>
> ### 51. How the Ladder Actually Decides
>
> Part X describes the rungs. This is the sender's controller, the bearer, sampled once per video frame — 24 a second
> by default. Down is cheap and fast; up is expensive and slow.
>
> The rungs today: L8 1920x1080 at 3 Mb/s, L7 1280x720 at 1.2 Mb/s, L6 854x480 at 600 kb/s, L5 640x360 at 300 kb/s in
> gray. Below L5 the picture shrinks: 480x360 at 250 kb/s, 320x240 at 150 kb/s, 160x120 at 60 kb/s, all gray. Video is
> VP8 (realtime, cpu-used 8, a keyframe at least every 2 seconds); a sender never sends a picture larger than its
> source.
>
> #### Down — any one of four
>
> The bearer steps down at once on any of these, checked in this order:
>
> Audio shed by the wire. The smallest, most protected thing on the plane being refused is the loudest congestion
> signal there is.
>
> More than three consecutive samples carrying drops, or a backlog of eight frames.
>
> More than five drops inside one second.
>
> More than three consecutive samples below the frame-rate floor, the greater of ten frames per second and 75 percent
> of the target. A rung that has just changed is not judged until 15 frames and 2 seconds have been sent at it.
>
> [keep "Why both a run count and a per-second rate?" and "Why a relative frame-rate floor" unchanged.]
>
> #### Up — both, or not at all
>
> [keep the three paragraphs and "There is deliberately no wall-clock step-rate cap" unchanged: 3 clean seconds at 98
> percent of the target rate, hold-off 15 s doubling to 300 s, forgiven after 60 s clean.]
>
> WATCH
> Bandwidth Experiments — the link starved by hand, both ends reading the same rung
>
> WORKED EXAMPLE — The ladder walked by hand, August 2026, the Python fnav Communicator
>
> [keep the 987 / 637 / 450 / 850 table and the two paragraphs after it, as history.]
>
> The controls that do this today are in the window: a Bandwidth slider and a Jitter slider. Bandwidth is your link,
> both directions. It runs from 32 kb/s to 4 Mb/s on a log scale, unlimited at the far right, and takes effect when
> the slider rests for 200 ms. It throttles your upload, so your bearer walks down and the others see a smaller
> picture of you, and it writes your MediaSpeed, so the media server sends you a smaller picture. Jitter, 0 to 500 ms,
> is also both directions: each arriving frame is delayed by a random 0 to N ms without reordering, and each frame you
> send waits up to N ms. Being able to starve your own call from inside the application is what makes the behaviour
> checkable by somebody who does not own a link shaper, and it is why Appendix J can ask a reader to reproduce it on an
> ordinary desk.
>
> Each sender burns its frame count and its wall clock at capture into the bottom-left of the picture, and the viewer
> shows its own clock beside it, so the lag is read off the screen: 64 ms feed to media server to window on one
> machine. COMMS_STAMP=0 turns it off.
>
> #### The floor is a voice
>
> Below L5 the picture is not thrown away; it gets smaller — 640x360, 480x360, 320x240, 160x120 — one step at most
> every 2 seconds, and only when the wire is refusing frames, because shrinking a picture helps only if the wire is the
> limit. It grows back one step after three clean 2-second windows, and when it is back at 640x360 the ladder resumes
> at L5. Only when 160x120 is refused too does the sender go audio only, and it stays there until the ladder is back
> at L5. Video is not gone; it is smaller. Audio is never what the ladder gives up.
>
> A video rung needs a camera, not a microphone. A camera with no microphone sends video with no audio bed, and the
> far end is told there is no audio.
>
> On one run — the window at 128 kb/s — it received 160x120 gray at 24 fps with audio continuous while its own sender
> walked down the ladder. Do not take that number as a specification. [keep the rest of the current "Do not take that
> number…" paragraph.]
>
> (Delete "The rule itself, in full" and "What it does when you drag it", or move them to §51a as the August design:
> "In August every participant derived one rate for the whole call from the same rows, with derive_rate. The rewrite
> replaced it: one upload, and the media server converts down per viewer.")

**REPLACEMENT — Specification C, in full (begin)**

# Specification C FD&F: Farinacci, Day & Fawcett

FD&F is the second transport specified by this Croakus. LISP locates, RINA names, and Ribbit holds the state and wakes the readers. Every member connects outward to one RAM manager, writes what it knows about itself there, and learns about the others only from there. Nothing is sent member to member. It began as a test of the boundary — if Ribbit is unchanged above both FrogNet and FD&F, the boundary has earned its name — and it is now also what FrogNet nodes join in place of the broker and the getHosts crawl.

WORKED EXAMPLE  —  FD&F: every member connects outward to the RAM manager; no member-to-member path through FD&F

### 62.  The Three Separations

FD&F began as a deliberate question: whether the architecture right for the real world is a hybrid of LISP, RINA and FrogNet. It came after lispers.net and rlite had each been converted onto Ribbit, and it took its present shape when the incidental plumbing of each was stripped away. LISP separates identity from location. RINA separates naming and scope from any one physical structure; in FD&F a DIF is a shared RAM, a region. Ribbit separates distributed computation from message choreography. With those three in place, much of the machinery each implementation carried moves to a lower layer or goes.

WORKED EXAMPLE  —  RINA names the thing; LISP locates it; Ribbit defines what programs do once attached

The name is literal: Farinacci, Day & Fawcett. It acknowledges the three lines of work whose separations meet here. It does not imply endorsement by Dino Farinacci or John Day; the architecture and the measurements stand on their own.

### 63.  The FD&F Participant Model

A member is an individual machine: a FrogNet node, a Linux or Windows computer, a phone. It may sit on a LAN, behind NAT, or anywhere on the Internet. It opens no inbound port.

Each member holds one FNWP session to its region's RAM manager (`rina-host`): three outbound TCP connections — requests, replies, and a data socket for large replies — framed FNW1 and compressed with BLDC-1 against the primed template store (`fdf.prime`). On the same 605 sends, FD&F's C++ BLDC-1 is byte-identical to FrogNet's Python BLDC-1.

Liveness is the session. Both ends set TCP keepalive (idle 2 s, three probes 1 s apart) and a 5,000 ms user timeout, so a silent loss is seen in about four seconds and a clean close in tens of milliseconds. When a session ends, everything it wrote goes with it: the announcement, the mappings, the leaf address, the where cells, the attention notices. Gone is gone.

Discovery is state. A member announces its name, its IPv4 addresses, its capability and its facts (a JSON object it keeps in `/opt/fdf/var/facts.json` and re-announces when the file changes). Capability is FrogNet's capability probe: a FrogNet node's daemon runs `frognet_capability_probe.sh` and publishes it as `facts.capability`, and every node elects the databasehost and mediahost from those facts; `fdf-client` on a plain machine reports cores, memory and OS under the same field names. The participant list is a query. Change is a held read. Nothing broadcasts on a LAN and nothing propagates neighbour to neighbour.

Applications talk only through the shared space. Ribbit programs and web pages read and write the region; page input goes in as `<App>.<QueueName>.<submitter>`. rlite's own programs run unchanged against `librina_region.so`, each SDU one request through the RAM manager. A qualification capture fails on any member-to-member application packet.

### 64.  The Six Transport Definitions

WORKED EXAMPLE  —  Ribbit binds to six transport definitions, not to a routing implementation

| Definition | FD&F answer |
|---|---|
| Locate | Given the area's name, the RAM manager's address: from its LISP mapping, or given directly. |
| Connect | The FNWP session to that RAM manager. |
| Announce | Name, addresses, capability and facts, written into the region (op `announce`). |
| Participants | The live announcements (op `participants`); a member whose session ended is not listed. |
| Capability | One participant's capability, from its announcement. |
| Change | A held wait that returns when the participant set changes (op `watch_participants`). |

No definition carries a route, a path or a neighbour (`src/transport.hpp`). That omission is normative. A transport that routes keeps its routing inside, as FrogNet does. A conformance client that knows only these six gets an answer to each (`transport-conformance`).

The FrogNet region (§66) does keep a graph of the network in the region, and FrogNet nodes route their own IP traffic from it. That graph is data FrogNet reads; it is not a definition Ribbit binds to, and the RAM manager routes nothing.

### 65.  Name, Location, and Session

RINA names things; LISP locates them.

Names. An area is a DIF. Inside it a machine is named by identity, `w:<guid>`, never by address, and a service says where it runs as `w:<guid>:<port>` in a where cell. Each reader resolves the identity in its own scope with the region's `resolve` op: a node's `.1`, a leaf's /32, or `ENOENT` when that member is gone. A where cell written with an address is not taken.

Locations. A FrogNet node's /24 is a LISP EID prefix. `fdf-client` registers it with a signed Map-Register (HMAC under the pond's site key) whose locator set names the hub. The region refuses a /24 that overlaps a live member's, and a mapping that a live session already holds, naming the holder both times. The hub installs a prefix only from a verified mapping that names it.

Bootstrap. A machine must be told one thing: where the shared space is. That is either the area's name — the Map-Resolver's address is then derived from the name by rule (SHA-256 of the name into a reserved prefix, bound anycast by whoever serves the area's mapping) — or the RAM manager's address directly. The FrogNet region uses the direct form: `fdf-region-join` defaults to `165.232.153.144:51888`, area `frognet`. The bootstrap is meant to be encrypted; today it is in the clear, and the only signature is the HMAC on mappings.

Recovery. A RAM-manager restart ends every session. Members reconnect, re-announce and re-register; nothing tells a neighbour, because FD&F has no neighbour relation. In name mode a member holds a read on the area's mapping and follows the RAM manager to its new address by itself. With a directly given address there is no mapping to follow.

### 66.  The Region, the Ponds, and the Bridges

The region. A FrogNet pond on FD&F is a region. On the droplet StreamingFrog (165.232.153.144): the RAM manager `rina-host` on TCP 51888 (`fdf-ram-frognet`, Permanent records in `/opt/fdf/var/area-frognet.perm`), the hub `fdf-hub` on UDP 50889 (`wg-frognet`, 10.253.253.254/16), and the hub's own membership (`fdf-client-frognet`). A node joins with one installer (`sudo bash /tmp/fdf-install-aarch64.run <SITE KEY>`, or the x86_64 one), which runs `fdf-region-join`: site.env, join.env, `fdf-client`, and the tunnel daemon restarted in region mode.

Each node writes its facts (`fdf-facts`: its addresses, the leases it holds and gives with their MACs, its uplink, its hub edge 10.253.A.B). Every node builds the same graph from everyone's rows (`fdf_graph.py`) and its daemon applies its own slice: LAN routes, a default toward the chosen exit, hub routes once the handshake is up, `/etc/hosts`, the elections. The hub applies its slice: one WireGuard peer per live member, AllowedIPs only from verified mappings, one owner per /24. Each region event is one reconcile; nothing polls.

That replaced the broker's pairwise tunnels (2·N(N−1)/2 interfaces) with one interface and N peers, and the getHosts crawl, makeHostJson and propagateNotification with one `world` read per event. Echo and aliveness are answered from the region. IP between nodes rides WireGuard to the hub on the RAM manager's host, never node to node across the Internet; LAN paths stay direct, which is FrogNet's own transport. A device off the mesh is a leaf: the region gives it the lowest free 10.252.X.Y/32, the hub peers it, and the nodes route and name it. Nobody types an address.

Ponds. A machine is in one forward pond and any number of participant ponds (`ponds/<area>.env`, one `fdf-client@<area>` each, the `forward` file names which). The forward pond gets the full facts, the routes, the names and the tunnel. A participant pond gets `{"role":"participant"}`, shared memory and attention notices — the tuple `(pond, attention, w:<guid>)`. `fdf-forward <area>` switches in place, without reconnecting. Each RAM manager publishes `(pond, config, net)` from its `net.json`: `reach` none, listed or all, and `map` allowed or refused. Members can read it and cannot write it. A node reaches another pond's services as that pond's config allows, on its own interface `rch-<h>`, mapping any destination that collides with its own addresses 1:1 into 100.64.0.0/10. A refused reach ends that pond's session.

The bridges. The phone and the PC are bridges: members of every pond they list at once, with one TUN. The phone runs the FrogNet member app (Kotlin) and `fdf-tun` (Go, wireguard-go): one VPN TUN at 100.127.255.1, one WireGuard device per pond, and the 1:1 rewrite at the IP header, including ICMP errors' quoted headers. On the phone all of 10/8 is the forward pond's, so every other pond is aliased. The Windows PC runs the same Kotlin member on a bundled Java runtime, `fdf-tun.exe` on a Wintun adapter, and netsh for its address and routes. An application asks for `w:<guid>:<port>`; exactly one network may answer, and two answers are refused as ambiguous. A released alias block returns to the same pond and prefix and to nothing else for 600 s. Addresses written inside application payloads are not rewritten; FrogNet's own applications name endpoints by identity, so they never carry one.

The Communicator on a region. A node runs `comms-ram` (8800) and `comms-media` (8994) with `fdf-comms.sh`, which writes the where cell `(pond, where, w:<guid>) {"comms":{"ram":"w:<guid>:8800","media":"w:<guid>:8994"}}`. The cell lives only while that node's session does. The desktop Communicator (`comms-app`, C++/Qt) gets its servers from the bridge (`fdf-bridge.cmd ask comms`); the Android Communicator is a screen of the FrogNet app, opened with its pond's servers.

### 67.  What Was Removed, and What Is Measured

The October 7 restart removed working machinery: member-to-member UDP/Ethernet data, route derivation in the agents, route-change resend, the NAT relay and its election, the LAN-join broadcast, host-change neighbour propagation, the Map-Resolver probe and election, the topology rig, and the experiments that existed to qualify them. The code worked. The boundary was wrong. Removing it took the per-machine agent from 784 lines to 327. Joining FrogNet to the region then removed, in region mode, the broker, the crawl, makeHostJson, the store's capability tuples and the merge pokes. A mechanism does not earn a layer because it is already written.

No fallbacks. A fallback that reaches around the architecture can turn a test green and invalidate the result. Qualification prints configured state, counts observed behaviour rather than exit codes, captures the wire, and fails on any member-to-member application packet.

Measured, in the sandbox:
- rlite's 30 integration tests pass with flow data through the RAM manager. Unfavourable: 1.03 requests and about 520 µs per transaction, against about 22 µs on the old member-to-member path.
- Three machines announce, are listed with name, IP and capability, and one that leaves is gone; 110 packets on the shared network, 0 between machines.
- Given only the area's name, three machines derive the same Map-Resolver address and join; after the RAM manager moves, all three re-attach by themselves in about 300 ms, with no packet between machines and no DNS packet.
- A flat single-unit send cut the frame from 111–117 bytes to 87–88 and the bytes out by 23 percent. The LISP mapping region agrees with lispers.net in seven of seven cases, and a 5-second TTL expires at about 5.1 s. Permanent records survive kill -9, and a reliable flow completes 200/200 across a RAM-manager restart.
- Unfavourable: FrogSim's 20 topologies pass 6 — after the restart FD&F reaches only where IP already routes to the RAM manager — and a machine that changes its address keeps a stale locator.
- The region, build r19-a15 in network namespaces: region-loop 20/20 (the Communicator and the phone's code included); reach 68/68; fdf-tun 20/20; ponds-daemon 21/21; hub 25/25; graph 17/17. A change on one node is replanned on the others in about 80 ms on a LAN. A member's death is seen in about 20 ms when clean and about 4 s when silent. All nodes name the same new databasehost within one event of its loss.

On hardware:
- The droplet and the FrogNet fleet (Seattle7, Seattle3B, Seattle5, HappyDog, New-York-2, AI-Host) ran the region through builds r5–r13; each fix in that range was found there.
- 2026-10-07: a Windows laptop behind a home NAT, every connection outward to the droplet, held an 850x480, 24 fps call at 400–800 kb/s through the droplet's RAM and media servers.
- 2026-10-10: the FrogNet app's Communicator ran on a physical phone as a leaf.
- 2026-10-11: a Windows 11 PC joined as a bridge, first on Seattle5's LAN, then from another network through the hub, and logged into a node through it.
- Not yet shown on hardware: the phone's bridge (`fdf-tun` on a handset), a second pond reached from the PC, and the join cost per member on the current build.

The bad numbers stay because qualification is not advertising. The question is first whether the abstraction is correct and transport-independent; performance follows a green contract.

WORKED EXAMPLE  —  The evidence discipline: incumbent behaviour is the oracle; measurements are data, including the bad ones

### 68.  Why FD&F Matters to Ribbit

FrogNet and FD&F disagree about transport internals. FrogNet discovers a changing physical graph, splits, heals and merges, and stays sovereign and offline-first. FD&F puts the meeting place at one reachable RAM manager and keeps routing out of its definitions. The same Ribbit programming surface runs above both, and FrogNet nodes now run their own discovery and tunnels on an FD&F region. The programming surface is therefore not an accident of FrogNet's topology.

WORKED EXAMPLE  —  One Ribbit programming model above transports that solve reachability in different ways

FOR THE GUILD
The point of FD&F is not that every network should look like FD&F. Once the upper programming surface stops depending on how the transport works, transports become replaceable answers to a lower question. FrogNet can be sovereign. FD&F can be manager-centred. An InfiniBand implementation can answer the same definitions its own way. Ribbit should not care.
Two doors. [keep the existing "Two doors" paragraph unchanged]

- **Evidence**: runMerge.bash:116-122,331-338 (region mode: makeHostJson skipped, autoswitch is the daemon's); poll.py run_region_loop (one reconcile per region event; no crawl, no propagateNotification); docs/HANDOFF-RAM-DISCOVERY-2026-10-09.md r15 (≈80 ms end to end on a LAN); fdf-region-join:2-9.
- **Fix**: add at the end of §60b's "Convergence" subsection: "On a node in an FD&F region none of this applies. A change reaches every node as one region event, in well under a second on a LAN; there is no neighbour-by-neighbour propagation, no makeHostJson and no merge notification. Its tunnel is one WireGuard channel to the hub; to re-assert it, run `sudo bash /tmp/fdf-install-aarch64.run <SITE KEY>` (or the x86_64 installer) again. Add `fdf-client` to the services to check."

**10.**
- **Where**: §61 Smaller Things — "Receive-side audio priority"
- **Text**: "On the way in, frames are handled in arrival order. The same rule should apply in both directions and currently does not."
- **Verdict**: INCONSISTENT / STALE
- **Evidence**: audio and video are separate planes with separate fan-out: server.cpp:89-95 (`PLANE_AUDIO` → fan_audio, video → fan_video), :105; Part XV §47 states two planes.
- **Fix**: delete the entry, or: "Both planes still share one interface queue on the way in, as on the way out; nothing schedules that queue."

**11.**
- **Where**: §61 "Region policy is not one universal consistency rule"
- **Text**: "Early FrogNet Memory commonly used last-write-wins plus age-out for current-state cells."
- **Verdict**: INCONSISTENT (settled doctrine)
- **Evidence**: settled ruling: FrogNet default Region holds current state, not history; not to be called last-write-wins.
- **Fix**: "FrogNet's default Region holds one value per coordinate — current state, not history — with age-out for current-state cells."

**12.**
- **Where**: FOR THE GUILD (end of §61)
- **Text**: "per-sender backpressure is information the relay already has and does not use; a headless publisher joining rather than originating is a session-shape change."
- **Verdict**: INCONSISTENT
- **Evidence**: the same §61 marks both as "Closed, 11 August 2026"; "Four of those edges are in the media path" counts closed items.
- **Fix**: "Read that list again as a work list. The edges still open are small and bounded and each has an obvious oracle — naming whose frame the media server discarded is information it already has. If you want a first contribution that is real rather than ceremonial, take one from here."

## 25_Part_XVIII_What_Follows_From_It.md

**13.**
- **Where**: §70 Ribbit Without FrogNet
- **Text**: "FD&F runs Ribbit over a transport that shares no machinery with FrogNet."
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: rina_node.cpp:157-173 announces FrogNet's capability probe verbatim; transport.hpp Participant.capability "FrogNet's capability probe, verbatim"; poll.py:2372-2400 the region's elections read the probe as facts.capability; FD&F's wire is FNW1 + BLDC-1 (frogram.cpp:48-50, semcodec.hpp), byte-identical to FrogNet's Python BLDC-1 on 605 sends (RESULTS.md:1421-1436); FrogNet nodes join FD&F regions (fdf-region-join; internet_tunnels_v3/region.py BUILD_TAG fdf-2026-10-11-r19-a15).
- **Fix**: "Specification C answers the first half: FD&F runs Ribbit without FrogNet's discovery, routing, elections, broker or tunnels. It shares FrogNet's wire — FNWP framing and BLDC-1, byte-identical on the same sends — and FrogNet's capability probe. It also runs the other way round: FrogNet nodes now join an FD&F region and take their membership, addresses and tunnels from it."

**14.**
- **Where**: §70 "Federation is the research question"
- **Text**: "The honest state of this: Ribbit does not do it, no design exists"
- **Verdict**: STALE (partly)
- **Evidence**: PLAN-PONDS.md / MERGE-r19-a12.md `[FORWARD_IS_A_FACT_V1]`, `[NET_CONFIG_IS_A_TUPLE_V1]`, `[REACH_V1]`: a machine is in several ponds at once, each its own memory; each pond's own config tuple decides what another may reach; colliding addresses are mapped 1:1. PLAN-FROGNET-ON-FDF.md "Relationships between DIFs": nesting and multiple membership designed; cross-region attach "not demonstrated". No memory merges or mirrors another.
- **Fix**: "The honest state of this: a machine can now be in several ponds at once, each pond its own memory, and reach into another as far as that pond's own configuration allows. That is membership in two memories, not two memories seeing parts of each other. Ribbit does not do the second, and no design for it is built. The parts of the problem that are hard…" (rest unchanged).

**15.**
- **Where**: §71 Other Substrates
- **Text**: "the C++ RAM host keeps a Region in memory and persists it to a plain disk file, with no MySQL, and FD&F's RAM manager is that host."
- **Verdict**: WRONG (wording)
- **Evidence**: TREE/opt/frognet_semantic/ribbit/cpp/src/ram_host.cpp:29 "The backing store is this application's choice (here: memory; a restart is an empty memory)"; persistence is FD&F's rina-host only: rina_region.cpp:42-49 `[REGION_FILE_V1]` Permanent records appended to RINA_REGION_FILE.
- **Fix**: "It now exists: FD&F's RAM manager, built on the C++ RAM host, keeps the Region in memory and appends its Permanent records to a plain disk file, with no MySQL."

**16.**
- **Where**: Part XVIII lead
- **Text**: "Everything before this part is built and running."
- **Verdict**: INCONSISTENT
- **Evidence**: Part XVI (23_…) precedes it and is the inventory of what is not built.
- **Fix**: "Everything in the parts before this one is built and running, except what Part XVI lists."

# Report J — Appendices A–F (files 26–31), against the 2026-10-11 tree

I had the conversation tools. TREE = /home/claude/tree1011. All file:line references below are relative to TREE unless they say otherwise. I re-checked every 10-08 lead for my Parts. Each one is still wrong in today's code, except where an entry below says otherwise. New findings are marked **NEW**.

---

## 26 Appendix A — The Capability Probe

**A1. Where**: 26, intro paragraph
- **Text**: "run once per boot and are cached; the cheap, time-varying signal is sampled fresh on every call."
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: usr/local/bin/frognet_capability_probe.sh:107-127. The `[NO_AVCAP_CACHE_V1]` docstring reads "Capability, computed. Every call, every merge." There is no cache. The comment at lines 22-23 contradicts itself; the code does not cache. Lines 352-363: if any field fails to measure, the probe prints nothing and exits 1, so the last good row stands.
- **Fix**: "Every measurement, the CPU benchmark and the disk write-and-fsync included, is taken fresh on every call. Nothing is cached. If any field cannot be measured, the probe emits nothing and exits non-zero, and the last good capability stands."

**A2. Where**: 26, "Cached fast, sampled fresh" (the whole section)
- **Text**: "so they run once per boot and are cached in /run/frognet_avcap.json"
- **Verdict**: REWRITE
- **Evidence**:
  - The probe has no cache (A1). `[NO_AVCAP_CACHE_V1]` retired /run/frognet_avcap.json after a cached lan_ip stayed stale for 2.9 days (probe.sh:110-123).
  - On an FD&F region the tunnel daemon runs the probe at start and then every FROGNET_CAP_REFRESH_SEC seconds (default 600, off the event loop). It writes `facts.capability` only when the slow-moving part changes; loadavg, temps_c, mem_available_kb, disk_free_gb and ts are left out of that comparison (opt/frognet_semantic/internet_tunnels_v3/poll.py:2372-2403, `[CAPABILITY_IS_A_FACT_V1]`).
- **Fix** (replacement section):
  > **Measured every time.** The probe takes every reading on every call, with no cache: a cached identity field once went stale for days. If any reading fails, the probe emits nothing and the last good capability stands. Capability rows do not age. On an FD&F region the tunnel daemon runs the probe when it starts and every ten minutes after that. It writes the result into the region as this member's `capability` fact, but only when the slow-moving part has changed. Load, temperatures, free memory and free disk are not announced.

**A3. Where**: 26, "What it returns" field list
- **Text**: "SSD / NVMe / rotational"
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: probe.sh:265-272 emits `nvme | ssd | hdd | sdcard | unknown`.
- **Fix**: "disk_class  nvme / ssd / hdd / sdcard / unknown"

**A4. Where**: 26, field list (**NEW**)
- **Text**: "(arm64, x86_64, i686, ...)"
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: probe.sh:149 uses `platform.machine()`, which reports `aarch64` on a 64-bit ARM Linux box such as a Pi.
- **Fix**: "(aarch64, x86_64, armv7l, ...)"

**A5. Where**: 26, field list (fields left out)
- **Text**: "The fields an election actually weighs:"
- **Verdict**: WRONG (incomplete)
- **Evidence**:
  - probe.sh emits `disk_write_mbps` (298), `disk_fsync_ms` (301), `lan_ip` (332), `av_port=9000` (333), `ffmpeg_path` (161), and `mysql_server`, `mysql_client`, `mysql_config` and `mysql_innodb_buffer_pool_size` (224-236).
  - `temps_c` appears only when thermal zones exist (341).
  - `cpu_mhz` is the cpufreq maximum, or the /proc/cpuinfo "cpu MHz" value when cpufreq is absent (135-146).
- **Fix**: Add `disk_write_mbps` and `disk_fsync_ms` under STORAGE. Add an IDENTITY group: `lan_ip` (this node's .1, from getFrogNet.bash) and `av_port` (9000). Note that `temps_c` appears only where the kernel exposes thermal zones.

**A6. Where**: 26, after the field list
- **Text**: "Note what is not here: there is nothing about the network."
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: probe.sh:306-332 publishes `lan_ip`. On a region, `region_capability.set_snapshot` overwrites it with the .1 the region knows (opt/frognet_semantic/core/region_capability.py:61-62).
- **Fix**: "Note what is not here: no reachability, no round-trip times, no routes. The one address in the blob is lan_ip, the node's own .1. That is its identity, not a network measurement."

**A7. Where**: 26, worked example (database host)
- **Text**: "The database host selector leans on cores, memory, disk_free_gb, disk_class, and the InnoDB pool"
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: opt/frognet_semantic/core/database_handler.py:59-174.
  - The gate is `mysql_running`.
  - The score is installed `mem_total_kb` + 0.5 × the InnoDB pool, plus 8 × the disk_class weight, plus cores × cpu_mhz.
  - The `disk_free_gb` floor is gone (`[NO_MOVING_INPUT_IN_A_STATIC_RANK_V1]`). `mem_available_kb` is never read.
- **Fix**: "The database host selector gates on mysql_running, then scores installed memory, the InnoDB pool, disk_class, and cores × clock. These are static properties only; nothing that moves with load is scored."

**A8. Where**: 26, worked example (media host)
- **Text**: "it weighs encoders, hw_encoder, gpu_render_node, and cpu_bench"
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: opt/frognet_semantic/core/sotf_handler.py:331-364.
  - The gate is `ffmpeg` and `libvpx`.
  - The ceiling is `cpu_bench_total`/1000, or cores × clock when no bench was published.
  - A hardware encoder multiplies the score by 2.5. A vaapi claim counts only if `gpu_render_node` is true.
  - Under 0.5 GB of RAM the score is halved.
  - The `encoders` list is never read.
- **Fix**: "The media host selector gates on ffmpeg with libvpx, then scores the measured CPU benchmark. A real hardware encoder (vaapi only with a render node) multiplies that by 2.5, and a box under half a gigabyte of RAM is halved."

**A9. Where**: 26, "One base probe, plus what each service adds" (the whole section)
- **Text**: "Each service's handler publishes its own <role>/capability tuple into the shared space"; "so the media handler's own probe checks for that and includes it in the tuple it publishes"
- **Verdict**: REWRITE
- **Evidence**:
  - Nothing checks for OpenCV (grep of core/, daemon/ and usr/local/bin finds only package_communicator.py's pip hint). Off a region, `role_publish.publish_all` probes once and has every role handler write the same blob (core/role_publish.py:44-79). There is no per-handler probe hook.
  - The only per-role addition is `av_port`, which frognet_register_candidate.sh:52-55 stamps for the mediahost.
  - On a region, `[CAPABILITY_TUPLES_OFF_IN_REGION_V1]` turns publish_all off (role_publish.py:60-66). The elections read members' `facts.capability` (core/region_capability.py:1-14, 47-69; discovery/live.py:676-685). fdf-region-join disables the 60 s advertiser timers (opt/fdf/bin/fdf-region-join:28-38).
  - The OpenCV claim came from a Claude edit, not from John.
- **Fix** (replacement section):
  > **One probe, read two ways.** There is one probe per node and no per-service probe. Off a region, each merge runs the probe once, and every role handler publishes that same blob as its `<role>/capability` tuple. The media role adds the A/V port it serves. The elections read those tuples back from the store. On an FD&F region the store is not an election input. The tunnel daemon writes the blob into the region as the member's `capability` fact, and every member's elections read the alive members' facts. That way the input does not live on the host that just died. Either way, no component hands another a blob. A handler that needs a fact the base probe does not take would have to add it to the probe. None does today.

## 27 Appendix B — The Echo Probe

**B1. Where**: 27, field table
- **Text**: "the wired address — the node's .1 identity, if eth0 is up"
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**:
  - usr/local/bin/getEth0Address returns `<prefix>.1` from eth0's 10.x address. With no such address it takes the prefix from the dnsmasq `dhcp-range`, so field 2 is always the .1.
  - getWlan0IP and getWlan1IP return the first and second entries of `FROGNET_UPSTREAM_DEVIPS`, or `0.0.0.0`. They never return an empty field.
  - Upstream entries are the real unicast addresses on interfaces other than eth0 that do not own a served .1. That excludes 10.253/16, 10.254/16, .1, link-local, loopback and multicast, and includes non-10.x and public addresses (usr/local/bin/mapInterfaces:104-133, 197-211).
  - Field 1 is `getOurDomain`, the dnsmasq `domain=` value (the node name). When that times out, field 1 is `hostname -s`, and that line is not cached (getFrogNet.bash:53-114).
- **Fix**:
  ```
  name,identity,upstream1,upstream2
  name       the node name (dnsmasq domain=)
  identity   the node's .1 — always present
  upstream1  first upstream address this node holds on a non-eth0 link, or 0.0.0.0
  upstream2  second upstream address, or 0.0.0.0
  ```

**B2. Where**: 27, worked example
- **Text**: "HomeBase.frognet,10.80.80.1,,"
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: The same code as B1. setup_lillypad_v4.bash:482 writes `domain=$NETWORK_NAME`. The empty fields read `0.0.0.0`.
- **Fix**: "HomeBase,10.80.80.1,0.0.0.0,0.0.0.0"

**B3. Where**: 27, after the example
- **Text**: "A node running as a wireless access point instead carries its identity in wlan0IP and leaves eth0IP blank"
- **Verdict**: WRONG
- **Evidence**: getEth0Address falls back to the dhcp-range prefix, so an access-point node still reports its .1 in field 2. Fields 3 and 4 are upstreams, not the access point.
- **Fix**: Delete the wired-versus-wireless sentence. Replace it with: "Field 2 is the node's identity whether it is wired or projecting an access point. Fields 3 and 4 say which addresses other networks gave it."

**B4. Where**: 27, last paragraph (**NEW**)
- **Text**: "The walk reads this to identify a freshly-probed address"; "so it is computed once and served from cache in well under a millisecond"
- **Verdict**: STALE (it describes broker mode only)
- **Evidence**:
  - On an FD&F region, var/www/html/frognet_echo.php (`[ECHO_FROM_THE_REGION_V1]`) builds the line from /opt/fdf/var/facts.json: the name, the `own` .1, and the `uplink` rows, or 0.0.0.0. It does not call getFrogNet.bash, and it prints nothing until the facts exist.
  - On a region the walk does not run (discovery/live.py:499-519, 661-662, `[NO_CRAWL_IN_REGION_V1]`). getHosts.php reads /var/lib/frognet/region_hosts.json.
- **Fix**: Add: "On an FD&F region the echo is the node's own facts, in the same four-field form, read from /opt/fdf/var/facts.json. There is no walk there. Who exists comes from the region's records."

## 28 Appendix C — Command & Helper Reference

**C1. Where**: 28, Install & identity, frognet_install.sh
- **Text**: "Flags: --name, --ip (10.X.Y.1), --broker-host, --broker-port, --no-ssid-projection, --ap-iface, --country."
- **Verdict**: WRONG (incomplete)
